# VOLUME 10, BATCH 0002 — Chapters 0461 to 0470, and this block wins over every block above it

**The ten faces below are the ten days' worth. The descriptors are the published ones and no batch may take a descriptor for anybody who does not already have one, and none was taken: fifty-seven occurrences of twelve descriptors across the ten files, and seven incidental figures who carry no number at all, and the map cannot reach any of the seven.**

### 1. The people in these ten days, and what each one did in them

- **ADRIAN VALE — Stage 2 on all ten days, aged nowhere, in three of the ten chapters and in no chapter in which he reads a page.** His hands are on **the frame of a barrow** in Chapter 0461, **the board off its nails, the bucket, the water and the trough** in 0464, and **the register-keeper's own slate, taken off her bench in the sixth hour and put back on it at the fourth hour, and a hazel stick off a gate he walked past, and eleven miles of wet ground** in 0466. **In 0461 and 0466 nobody asks him anything, nobody declines to ask him anything and nobody is waiting for him to be useful, and in both chapters he works something out and the chapter does not say he was right.** In 0461 he counts what goes on the outside edge of a tally-board he can see twice and what a man says out loud, and finds the two are not the same number, and has no way of putting the second beside the first and does not know the second is a number. In 0466 he says twice, to the flats, that there are places with nobody in them and places with three people in them and he has counted the same thing in both. In 0464 he is asked one ordinary question, which is where the trough stands, and moves it a hand's width with his foot, and says one sentence, and nobody answers him and nobody thanks him. **He performs no working, no threshold is opened, no workway is offered and he asks for none, and *passage* and *privilege* are at zero across the ten.**
- **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-NINE WHO KEEPS A PUBLIC REGISTER — her second slate, her third column empty on purpose.** She is in four of the ten. In 0463 she puts both hands flat on the table on either side of the sheet and **does not say one word about the difference between the two figures, and does not say the woman's sentence again, and does not agree with it out loud and does not disagree with it out loud.** In 0467 she says one sentence — that a thing carried is a thing somebody else decides what happens to, and a thing on a table is a thing nine people in a room can be wrong about together, and the second of those is worth more and costs more and she was not going to say what it costs that morning. In 0468 she hears a right figure said out loud in her room and says that she is not going to write it down and gives a reason about a hand and not about a board. In 0470 she answers a question nobody asked her in one sentence: **that there is no instrument in four hundred miles for asking a room to be asked anything in advance of anything.** **The register is not opened on any of the ten days, the second slate is not touched, and no hand but hers goes near that shelf.**
- **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-EIGHT AT THE SALT WHARF — a tally-board, a knife with a blunt edge, a barrow, and a strip of clear board about as long as a cart.** In 0461 he says about four hundred and forty out loud to nobody, and says that there are two sets of notches on that board, one for what he sends and one for what he has got, and that he does not say the second one out loud, and that nobody has asked him what the second one is. **Nothing in that chapter connects his answering on day 357 to his refusing on day 393, he does not say he knows what he said, and neither speech is repeated.**
- **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-NINE WHO TRADES ON A BOARD — a board.** In 0463 he says the thing that is said and is not thanked. In 0464 he reads another man's slate from where he is standing and says about nine words about a figure with nobody under it, and the man at the trestle says that is only his stall, and he says he is not saying a word about the stall and the two sentences are not the same sentence. **He is not thanked and the chapter does not say he was right. In 0470 he asks the room to put a figure on the store's board in chalk in the open, and nobody answers him, and the room empties, and he carries his own board out himself.** He is not defeated, is not converted and does not take his own board down.
- **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO KEEPS A STALL — a piece of chalk in his own inside breast pocket that he has not taken out since the forty-third day of the Short Month, which is day 298.** In 0468 his hand goes to that pocket about once a minute and comes back out without anything in it, and the day is printed at full length in that chapter. He is in five of the ten and **he is not asked about the ninth line in any of them and he is not thanked in any of them.**
- **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-TWO — a door she is not asked to open.** In 0463 she is the one who says the volume's finding, once, and does not say it again, and says before she starts that she is not going to be thanked, and is thanked by nobody, and nobody asks her where she got it from. **The finding that a household's days are not a rota is not put in her mouth in any of these ten chapters and is not improved on by anybody.** She is in 0467 and 0468 and is asked nothing in either.
- **THE MAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-SEVEN — a bucket and a rag, and a wall he has decided not to wash.** He is in two of the ten and **nobody asks him anything in either.** In 0464 he washes the store's board himself and says not one word to the man holding nothing. **The words he is going to speak on day 486 and the rule he is going to refuse on day 490 are in no chapter of these ten, and no chapter of these ten puts those two acts anywhere near each other, and no chapter of these ten washes the wall above the board.**
- **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE WHO CARRIES THINGS FOR A LIVING — a satchel with a flap.** In 0467 he is asked what is in it and he answers: a thing read out to nine people in a room and four people in a market, which he has not opened, and he would like nobody in that room to thank him for saying so. He names the five notices he carried in a coat once and says he has never opened one of them, and the return, and the last sheet that came up that road, and this satchel. **Four things in three volumes and the fourth is the last of them in this flood, and he is not thanked and he is not argued with.**
- **THE MAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-SEVEN — a length of new rope he has never used.** In 0462 he is in the room over a market with the rope over the back of his chair. **Nobody asks him where the fence is, nobody tells him, the rope is not used, is not cut, is not taken up and does not fall in this batch, and he walks out of that room with his own two hands and the rope stays on the back of his chair where it was all morning.** He is right about most of what he says and is not asked about any of it. **Whether the fence stands between the right two things is not settled and is left open in the chapter's own words.**
- **THE MAN OF ABOUT SIXTY-THREE WHO FARMS THE HALF OF THE HOLDING — a spade.** He comes in out of a wet ditch with the mud to the knee, says four words, stands in a doorway, and goes out. **Nobody asks him anything and the man of about fifty-seven is not asked about him.**
- **THE MAN OF ABOUT SIXTY-FOUR WHO DIGS SOMEBODY ELSE'S DITCH ABOUT NINE HUNDRED PACES OFF AND IS NOT PAID.** Four words, once, in Chapter 0466, to the ground and not to anybody. **He is not asked anything, and Adrian Vale says he did not ask him whose it was and is not going to.**
- **THE MAN OF ABOUT SIXTY, WITH THE HANDCART — a hazel mallet with its head bound in cord and a coil of rope.** In 0465 he brings the cart into a market, stands it against a wall, and says the count of what he built: **sixteen willow posts and eleven withies, the eleventh the one that took longest because the wood was in the wet, about four feet and he did not measure it and neither did anybody else.** **Nobody asks him why he put it up and nobody asks him whose it was, and about four people at that end of that market worked out since that he has been somewhere nobody there has been, and not one of them is going to say a word about it.**
- **THE MAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-NINE WHO WALKS THE FOUR-HUNDRED-MILE ROAD — a hurdle he made and a bar he replaced.** In 0469 he cuts a lashing on a hurdle with a clasp knife that is not sharp enough, and makes it again. He says he has walked that road four times in nine years, that it is four hundred miles, that he has never walked it for anybody and has never been asked to, and that he would like the record left blank anyway. **Nobody asks him where he has been, nobody asks him what he wants, nobody asks him about anybody, and he does not talk about anybody, and he is not thanked for the bar and not asked about the four times he has walked.**

### 2. The people who are not in these ten days and were not expected to be

**THE WOMAN WHO WALKED A MARKET ON TWO FEET IS AT ZERO, AND THE REASON IS PUBLISHED IN THE BATCH RECORD AND IN THE OPEN-THREADS FILE: Chapter 0466 is a count made on foot and she is not on it, because a chapter that puts her on a count turns the person who used this manuscript's missing instrument into a document.** **TAMSIIN QUILL is not in these ten chapters, so the two women of twenty-nine are never in one room and the consent fracture is neither mended nor visible and the fifth condition is not given. JONAH PIKE is not in this volume.** The man of about fifty-two who digs is not in this volume. **The woman of about sixty-nine is not in these ten chapters and does not ask a fourth question, and no chapter of these ten remarks on her or mentions her.** The woman of about fifty-four is not in these ten chapters and nobody asks her where her line is and the round figure that falls on day 470 is not printed.

### 3. The instrument rule that binds every name and every face above, and it is the last line of this block

**NO NAME AND NO PLACE ARRIVED BY NARRATION IN THESE TEN CHAPTERS. EVERY CARD NAMED THE DESCRIPTOR OF EVERY PERSON IN IT AND EVERY CHAPTER MATCHED ITS CARD'S CAST, and the batch added no person and took no descriptor from anybody. THE POOL IS EMPTY MEASURED ON ALL FOUR HUNDRED AND FIFTY FILES, AND THE NINE INCIDENTAL FIGURES WHO CARRY NO NUMBER ARE NAMED IN THE BATCH RECORD AND ARE NOT IN THE CAST.** **AND THE THING THIS BATCH'S OWN RECORD GOT WRONG ON A FIRST PASS AND CORRECTED BEFORE IT WAS FINISHED, published here because the same failure has happened twice in this volume already: the *board* marker returns twenty-five occurrences across the ten chapters and only five of them are the store's board, and the first pass of the record listed Chapter 0461 as naming it, and Chapter 0461 does not name it — those ten occurrences are wharf planks, and a row that says a chapter names an object it does not name is a row a later phase will inherit as fact. A marker that cannot see is an instrument and not a filter, and the object inventory's third column is a plan and not a record, and every departure from it is published in the batch record and none was repaired.**

**AND THE ONE THING NO FILE THIS BATCH WROTE MAY PRINT: the name of Volume 09 and the words the man of about thirty-four says in Chapter 0428; the volume's one rule, said on day 438; the answer spoken in Chapter 0357; the refusal spoken in Chapter 0393, whose six words also stand at `bible/power-system.md:1684` and at `outline/volume-08.md:208`; and the day-154 sentence. NAMED WHERE EACH OF THEM IS, PRINTED NONE OF THEM, NO ABSENCE CERTIFIED. THE HOLD IS ON THIS BATCH'S OWN FILES AND NOT ON WHERE A STRING STANDS: a plan is not a page and a permanent witness section is not a page either.**

## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0002 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS, and this block is at the foot of this file and it wins over every block above it

**A reviewer returned six blocking and three moderate findings on Chapters 0461 to 0470. Nothing was restarted, no person was re-described, no descriptor was taken and no age moved. The one finding in this file's subject matter was a record asserting a property of a character that the page contradicted, and it was the record that was wrong.**

### 1. THE ONE CHARACTER-PROPERTY CORRECTION, and it is the one that mattered

**ADRIAN VALE'S SLATE IN CHAPTER 0466 IS NOT HIS, AND THE FIRST EDITION OF THIS RECORD'S OWN ENTRY SAID IT WAS.** The page says it twice and says it in the same chapter: `chapter-0466.md:3` — *he had neither of them from anybody: the slate was on a bench in the room over a market* — and `chapter-0466.md:31` — *The slate went on the bench in the room over a market with the marks on it and the cold still on his hands*. **He borrows the register-keeper's own working slate off her bench in the sixth hour, does not ask, and puts it back on the same bench at the fourth hour before the room fills. The reason in him is his own; the instrument is not. The two are separate and one record had them in one clause.** **IT IS NOT THE SECOND SLATE.** That slate is a third object, it is on the shelf behind the bench, it has a date on it and nothing under the date, and on day 466 it is not picked up, not turned over, not written on and no hand but the register-keeper's goes near that shelf. **A later batch that puts a hand on that bench for a second count takes this slate, and a batch that takes the other one has broken a prohibition by accident.**

**AND THE FAULT CLASS, because a record asserting a character's property is a different kind of error from a record asserting a count: every check this volume runs tests a count, an object or a prohibition, and not one of them tests whether a record agrees with a page about a thing that belongs to a person.** That is how six words survived a review that ran inside the phase before the record was finished. It is named at `outline/volume-10.md` §20.9.

### 2. WHAT DID NOT CHANGE ABOUT ANY PERSON

**No age moved. No descriptor was taken and none was attached to anybody. No person was invented, renamed, given a second handle, merged with anybody or split. The pool stays empty and `outline/volume-10.md` §19.5's measurement of it stands.** Adrian Vale is in three of the ten and in no chapter in which he reads a page. The man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board is thirty-nine and carries his own board out of that room in 0470. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall still has a piece of chalk in his own inside breast pocket that has not been out since the forty-third day of the Short Month, which is day 298, and nothing in this pass touches that pocket. The man of about fifty-seven is not asked, is not told, is not defeated, and his length of new rope is still over the back of his chair and was not used, not cut, not taken up and did not fall. The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register still has a third column she leaves empty on purpose and a second slate on a shelf behind her. The woman of about fifty-two still has the one place under a heading and is still not thanked. The man of about thirty-one still has a satchel with a flap and has not opened it. The man of about fifty-nine still has a hurdle and a bar he put in himself. The man of about sixty still has a hazel mallet with its head bound in cord. The man of about twenty-seven still has a bucket, a rag, and a wall he does not wash. **The woman who walked a market is still at zero across these ten chapters and still has no number and no descriptor, and the reason she is not on a count made on foot is at §16 item 9 and is unchanged.**

### 3. THE ONE PLAN FINDING ABOUT HIM, and it is about the shape of the next thirty chapters and not about these ten

**`outline/volume-10.md` §17.19 now states, as a rule, that "nobody asks him anything" is the pressure of this volume and not the reward, and that in every one of the fifteen chapters he is in he must be the cause of at least one thing that happens to somebody else** — a consequence that may not be a decision, a figure, a document, a notice or the panel. **The reviewer's finding is that the not-asking had become a reward condition across an unspecified number of volumes, and that a chapter in which nothing happens to another person because of him is a chapter that has spent the volume's one asset on nothing. In 0461 and 0466 he already is the cause of something; 0464 is the thin one, and the thinness is a plan call — the plan put the trough in the man of about twenty-seven's hands and Adrian in a foot's width of it — and §17.19 is the fix and it is a plan fix.** Nobody thanks him, nobody tells him he was right, nobody is waiting for him to be useful, and he is not useful to them, and §17.19 says the moment that becomes a reward he is a man being congratulated for being left alone. **He is still Stage 2 on all ten days, aged nowhere, and the fifteen and the fifteen-chapter column in §14.3 are untouched and no sixteenth was added.**

## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0003 — nine days, and one chapter in that span that is not on disk

### 1. What each person did, and what it cost them

**ADRIAN VALE — Stage 2, in three of the nine chapters and in no chapter in which he reads a page.** 0471: both hands on the frame of a barrow and then on a string and a board, in wind, and he gets a board that has come off a stall string flat on a barrow and the string is left hanging with its stone on the flags. 0476: both hands on a wet rag that is not his, taken off a woman's own stall board in front of about four people, wrung out over the trough and put back on the store's stone lip, and then the bucket, the standpipe and the water. 0479: both hands under the far end of the trough, a flat stone worked out of the store wall with his foot, and the trough let down onto it. **He says nothing at all in all three. Nobody asks him anything, nobody decides not to ask him, nobody is waiting for him to be useful, and he is not useful to them. In 0479 he works out that the flags under the far end of a trough are why a string cannot go back up, and the chapter does not say he was right.** **In all three he is the cause of something that happens to somebody else, and none of the three is a decision, a figure, a document, a notice or the panel.** The slate on the flats in Chapter 0480 is NOT his — it is the register-keeper's own working slate off her bench, and Adrian is not in that chapter at all.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE WHO CARRIES THINGS FOR A LIVING — a satchel with a flap.** He is in four of the nine chapters. In 0471 he stands at the end of a row and says to the wind that the road out of here is shut and there are three barrows on it four hundred miles up, that he has been up and down that road four times in a year with things he has not opened, and that not one man in that market has ever been out to the gate to look. In 0472 he says there is no day the round comes on any more than there was a week ago, and that seven sheets have gone up that road in this flood as notices and there is no eighth one, and that he carried five of the seven on his own back before any of them were keeping anything in that room. **In 0477 he puts the satchel on a bench in that room and leaves it there for a whole morning with the flap down, and names the six things he has carried and not opened, and says he will not be praised for that because a man who has not opened a thing has not done anything at all.** Then he says the thing this volume is built out of: **that he has knocked at more doors in this flood than anybody in this city and nobody has ever asked him a single thing while he was doing it, and that the reason is not that he is no use, it is that a man who knocks has already come to the door, and there is nobody in four hundred miles who can go out to a door to ask whether it would be all right.** Nobody in that room asked him what he knocked on, or how many, or what he found out, and he picks the satchel up at the eleventh hour and goes down the stair. **In 0480 he is the man who walks the flats, and he knocks at every door, and he comes back with about four hundred and thirty on the register-keeper's own slate with the first count's about four hundred and forty still on the same face of it and neither rubbed out.** He says twice into the weather and nobody hears either: that a man who knocks gets a different figure from a man who does not and the difference is in about ten places where he knocked and nobody came and he cannot tell whether anybody was in any of them; and that both of these are a count of places, neither is a count of people, there is nothing in four hundred miles that will turn one into the other, and he has two different numbers about the same ground and no way of knowing which is right and he is not going to pick one. **Nobody asked him to do it and he agreed to nothing and the reason in him is his own and the instrument is not.**

**THE WOMAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-NINE WHO KEEPS A PUBLIC REGISTER — her second slate, a third column she leaves empty on purpose.** She is in five of the nine. In 0472 she says that a room has got as far as a place where a man would have to stand and that no instrument in four hundred miles will tell a room which end to send him to. **In 0473 the volume's discovery about a book is in her mouth and is hers alone: a column that is empty on purpose and a column that is empty because there is nothing to put in it are two different things, and this room has been treating them as one, and the reason nobody has found that out is that she has never once had a figure to put in that column, and on the morning she has one the second column stops being a thing she is protecting and becomes a thing she is filling.** Nobody thanks her, nobody improves on it, nobody asks her where she got it from, and **the word the volume turns on is not in her mouth here and is not in any of these nine chapters at all.** In 0474 she says that two things leaned against a wall in that room are two things nobody is going to write on, and that the fact that they are in there that morning is the only reason anybody in this city is going to see them together. In 0477 she says one sentence — that he is the only man in this city who has been to the outside of a door in this flood and has nothing to show for it, that she has a shelf behind that bench that has the same trouble, and that she is not going to compare the two of them in front of about nine people. In 0478 she wipes her slate and puts it back and writes nothing on it. **She says in Chapter 0472 that there is no instrument in four hundred miles for asking anybody anything in advance of anything, and in 0477 that asking is the half of it she has never been able to do, and the decision that is hers, on a Sunday, is in a chapter that is not on disk.**

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO KEEPS A STALL — a piece of chalk he does not use.** He is in five of the nine. In 0471 he has a piece of chalk in his own inside breast pocket that he has not taken out since the forty-third day of the Short Month, which is the two hundred and ninety-eighth day of this flood, and his hand goes to that pocket about once a minute and comes back out with nothing in it, and his board comes off its string in the wind and goes onto a trestle and does not go back up that day, and he says a price said out loud and a price chalked on a board are not the same animal, and nobody thanks him and nobody says he was right. **In 0472 he says the sentence this thread now turns on: that he would go and make the other number himself, with a slate, this week, asking nobody for anything, and that he is not going to say it a second time in that room. Nobody accepted it and nobody refused it and he did not go.** In 0474 he comes up the stair with his own board for the first time in this flood and stands it against a wall and says nothing at all about the other man's board. In 0476 he is two stalls along and looks at the stone lip and looks away again. **In 0479 he puts his string back up on its post, his board back on the string and his stone on the foot of it on flags that are dry where the water has been walking across them, and says three sentences — that a board on a string wants dry flags under the foot of it as much as it wants anything else, that he is not saying a word to anybody about the trough, that the way you tell somebody something in this city is to wait until you already know and then say it, and that is not the same. **THE FIRST EDITION OF THIS SENTENCE SAID *FLAGS THAT ARE DRY FOR THE FIRST TIME IN A FORTNIGHT*, and a fortnight is an elapsed count of days, which this volume's rule does not print, and it was also not true: his string came down off that post on day 471 and this chapter is day 479, and 479 less 471 is eight. The page never printed a span and the page is what is right. The finding is at §5.1 row 3 of `state/batch-summaries/volume-10-batch-0003.md`.**** **He is not thanked and he is not told he was right in any of the five, and the ninth line is not mentioned, moved, dated, read aloud, thanked for or resolved in any of them.**

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-NINE WHO TRADES ON A BOARD — a board.** He is in five of the nine. In 0471 he comes the length of the row and does not stop, which is what he does at that end of that market. In 0473 he says the thing that is said. **In 0474 he says that he has carried a board in four markets for nine years and has never once put it in a room and has never once seen another man's board in a room, and that he does not know what a room does with two of them and is not going to find out by standing there looking at them; and the two men are not compared in that chapter in any mouth, and he goes down the stair and does not come back up it.** In 0476 he does not stop and does not look at the wall. **In 0478 he comes up the stair at the eleventh hour and stands at the back of that room and asks nothing at all, and looks at the length of new rope over the back of a chair, and neither of the two men says one word.** He is right in four seconds in Chapter 0485 and is not thanked and not argued with and the chapter does not say he was right. **He took a name off his own board in his own hand in about the ninth year of this flood and has never said so to anybody and says nothing about it in any of these nine chapters.**

**THE MAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-SEVEN WHO WASHES THAT BOARD — a bucket and a rag, and a wall he has decided not to wash.** **He is in three of the nine chapters — 0471, 0476 and 0479 — he is named by his descriptor once, and nobody asks him anything in any of the three, and no chapter of these nine prepares, guesses at or alludes to the day in Chapter 0486 when he is asked what he is doing to that board, and no chapter of these nine says one word about the fact that nobody has ever asked him.** In 0471 he is a rag on a stone lip because he put it there. In 0476 he is the box of chalk on the step and the shut lid, and a wet rag that is his going back to his stone lip. In 0479 he comes out of the door with the rag over his shoulder, looks at the stone under the end of the trough, goes back inside, and shuts the door without a word. **He speaks the name of this volume on day 486 and refuses this volume's one rule on day 490 and is thanked for neither and has asked in advance not to be, and neither of those acts is anywhere in a chapter of this batch and no chapter of this batch brings them near each other.**

**THE MAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-SEVEN — a length of new rope he has never used.** He is in ONE chapter of the nine and is asked nothing in it by anybody, including by the man who asked a room four days earlier. **He says the count — sixteen willow posts and eleven withies, the withies bound with the same cord, a man who cut them with a handcart he brought up the shut road and nobody sent him, and in about forty years not one person in this city has come out and asked him where the whole of it stands — and nobody improves on it, and then he says that the only thing anybody has ever done about a piece of ground in this city is put a number on something, and that he has had a piece of ground for about forty years with two men on it who have never agreed about it, and that nobody has ever put a number on anything at all about it, and that he does not know what that means and is not going to guess.** He is right about most of what he says and is not thanked and the chapter does not say he was right. **The rope is not cut, not taken up, does not fall, no hand in that chapter lifts it, and it is on the chair when he goes out and he leaves it there. Nobody asks him where the fence is and nobody tells him and whether the fence stands between the right two things is left in the chapter's own words and is not settled.**

**THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-TWO — a door she is not asked to open.** She is in ONE chapter of the nine, at the far end of the bench, and is asked nothing in it. **She does not say the volume's finding in these nine and the finding stays where Chapter 0463 put it, in her own mouth, and is not improved on and is not in a second mouth. The finding that a household's days are not a rota is not in her mouth in any of these nine.** In 0473 she takes her hands out of her lap and lays them flat on the bench on either side of her, and that is the whole of what the room does about the thing that was just said.

**THE FIGURES WITH NO DESCRIPTOR, and no descriptor was attached to any of them, and this is the same convention the two batches behind this one used.** A woman with a stall two along from the store, who is in Chapter 0471 and Chapter 0476 and is given no number in either and **is the one who names the cost of a figure on a wall out loud, in her own mouth, in a market**. A woman at a stall two along, in Chapter 0479. A man at the end of the row, in Chapter 0471 and Chapter 0476. A woman at the back of that room, in Chapter 0474. A man at the back of that room who works a method out on the fingers of his left hand with the thumb of his right and gets as far as the first part of it, in Chapter 0472 and in Chapter 0478. A man at the foot of the stair, in Chapter 0472. **EIGHT INCIDENTAL FIGURES ACROSS THE NINE, none of them with a number, none of them asked anything, none of them thanked, and none of them in the plan's cast. Two of them are the same woman by description and are not stated to be the same woman and nothing in these nine chapters turns on whether they are.**

### 2. The people who are not in these nine days and were not expected to be

**THE WOMAN WHO WALKED A MARKET ON TWO FEET IS AT ZERO, AND CHAPTER 0480 IS A COUNT MADE ON FOOT ACROSS ELEVEN MILES OF FLATS AND SHE IS NOT ON IT, and the reason is published in the batch record and is an instrument and not a decision about her: a chapter that puts her on a count turns the person who used this manuscript's missing instrument into a document.** TAMSIIN QUILL is in none of these nine chapters, so the two women of twenty-nine are never in one room and the consent fracture is neither mended nor visible and the fifth condition is not given. **The man of about thirty-eight at the salt wharf is in NONE of these nine chapters and *thirty-eight* is at ZERO in all nine, and so is the man who came up a lane with a barrow of boards, and the two are still two people.** The man of about sixty with the handcart and the hazel mallet is in none of these nine and his sixteen posts and eleven withies are carried in Chapter 0478 by a different mouth and are not attributed to him. **The man of about sixty-four who digs somebody else's ditch about nine hundred paces off is in none of these nine and the plan's *about nine hundred paces* is at ZERO here, and the plan gives him four lines in this volume and they are not in this batch.** The man of about fifty-two who digs is not in this volume. **The woman of about sixty-nine is in none of these nine chapters and does not ask a fourth question and no chapter of these nine remarks on her or mentions her.** The woman of about fifty-four is in none of these nine and nobody asks her where her line is. The girl of seventeen, the keeper of the Redroot gate and the Regent are at ZERO on both readings.

### 3. The instrument rules that bind every name and every face above

**NO NAME AND NO PLACE ARRIVED BY NARRATION IN THESE NINE CHAPTERS. EVERY CARD NAMED THE DESCRIPTOR OF EVERY PERSON IN IT, NO CARD COULD NAME ONE FROM THE PLAN'S TABLES, AND NO CHAPTER INVENTED A PERSON. THE POOL IS EMPTY MEASURED ON ALL FOUR HUND AND FIFTY FILES, AND THE EIGHT INCIDENTAL FIGURES WHO CARRY NO NUMBER ARE NAMED ABOVE AND ARE NOT IN THE CAST.**

**AND THE THING THIS BATCH'S OWN RECORD AND ITS OWN INSTRUMENT GOT WRONG, PUBLISHED HERE BECAUSE THE SAME FAILURE HAS HAPPENED IN EVERY BATCH OF THIS VOLUME: the *board* marker returns fifty-six occurrences across the nine chapters and only three of them are the store's board, and the first pass of this batch's own record listed Chapters 0473 and 0474 as naming it and neither of them does — a word-by-word reading removed both. The *box* marker returns six, all six of them in Chapter 0476, and every one of them is the store's own chalk box on the step, which the plan itself names, and the shut box of the flood is in none of the nine. And a duplication check with one threshold returned a clean batch that had nine sentences in it standing in for another book's sentences, and all nine were rewritten before the record was finished.** A marker that cannot see is an instrument and not a filter, and the object inventory's third column is a plan and not a record, and every departure from it is published in the batch record and none was repaired.

**AND THE CHARACTER-PROPERTY RULE, WHICH THE REVIEW NAMED AND WHICH THIS BATCH OBSERVED RATHER THAN ASSUMED: every check in this volume tests a count, an object or a prohibition, and not one of them tests whether a record agrees with a page about a thing that belongs to a person. The one property this batch held hardest is the slate. It is the register-keeper's own working slate in Chapter 0480, taken off her bench without asking and put back on the same bench, and it is NOT the second slate on the shelf behind that bench, and the chapter keeps the two apart in one sentence so that a later batch can see the separation on the page and not only in a record. A slate of a man's own is furniture. A slate off somebody's bench is a small theft nobody notices and that no chapter of this batch calls a theft.**

**AND THE ONE THING NO FILE THIS BATCH WROTE MAY PRINT: the name of Volume 09 and the words the man of about thirty-four says in Chapter 0428; the volume's one rule, said on day 438; the answer spoken in Chapter 0357; the refusal spoken in Chapter 0393, whose six words also stand at `bible/power-system.md:1684` and at `outline/volume-08.md:208`; the day-154 sentence; and the words the man of about twenty-seven speaks in Chapter 0486. NAMED WHERE EACH OF THEM IS, PRINTED NONE OF THEM, NO ABSENCE CERTIFIED. THE HOLD IS ON THIS BATCH'S OWN FILES AND NOT ON WHERE A STRING STANDS: a plan is not a page, a permanent witness section is not a page, and a batch record is not a page either.**

## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0004 — ten days, 0481 to 0490, and the name is spoken and the rule is refused

### 1. What each person did, and what it cost them

**ADRIAN VALE — Stage 2, in three of the ten chapters and in no chapter in which he reads a page.** 0482: both hands on a door's edge and its lintel, a bar off a leaning pole worked into a swollen jamb, and a piece of slate prised out of the top step and set on the flags so that a door stands open on its own. 0485: both hands on the frame of a barrow of stall boards, going out along a row and back. 0488: both hands under the end of a plank on a wet chute, then one hand under the frame of a shed door at the middle rail, and then a shoulder under that door while the other man gets the pins out. **He says nothing at all in all three chapters and the batch contains no quoted word of his. Nobody asks him anything, nobody decides not to ask him, nobody is waiting for him to be useful, and he is not useful to them. In 0488 he works out that a plank running down a chute will take a door frame out if the frame is square to the run of it, and the chapter does not say he was right. In all three he is the cause of something that happens to somebody else, and none of the three is a decision, a figure, a document, a notice or the panel.** He is not in 0483, 0484, 0489 or 0490, and the slate on the flats in the batch behind is NOT his.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-SEVEN WHO WASHES THE STORE'S BOARD — a bucket, a rag, and a wall he has decided not to wash.** **He is in TWO of the ten chapters, 0486 and 0490, and those two are four days apart and no sentence in either of them or in any of the other eight connects them.** In 0486 he comes up the stair with a bucket and a rag, sets the bucket on the step, folds the rag on the sill, wipes his hands down his coat and stands at the back of that room. He is asked one question, out loud, in front of about nine people, by the woman of about twenty-nine, and the question is what he is doing to that board, and nobody had ever asked him. **He answers once, in the local language, out loud, in that room, in that light, in four to eight words, and what he says is the name of this volume, and the words are on that page and in no file in this repository. Nobody thanked him for it, nobody improved on it, nobody asked him what he meant, nobody said it back to him, and not one of the nine wrote a word of it on anything.** In 0490 he is standing at the trough with the rag in his hand when the wall says its one thing, and the refusal is out of him in about four seconds and it is that he has washed those figures every morning since the seventh week of this flood, that he has not asked anybody to check them and nobody else has either, and that the reason they have stood up on that wall is not him and is not his washing, it is that he has not washed the wall above them. Nobody thanked him and nobody improved on it and he has asked in advance not to be thanked and has not been. **He is not named in the other eight chapters, is asked nothing in any of them, is referred to in no other of them, and no chapter of these ten says or half-says that he is about to be asked about anything. He is Stage 2 in no chapter because he is not a person the System has anything to say about, and the words *System*, *level*, *stat*, *points*, *mana* and *skill* are at zero across all ten files.**

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO KEEPS A STALL — a piece of chalk he does not use, in his own inside breast pocket.** He is in five of the ten chapters, the most of anybody. In 0481 he carries the store's bucket up a row twice and puts the water in that trough and will not say so and does not want the man who washes the board to find out from him. In 0485 he is two stalls along with a board on a string and a piece of chalk in that pocket **that he has not taken out since the forty-third day of the Short Month, which is the two hundred and ninety-eighth day of this flood, printed at full length**; he hears the man of about thirty-nine be right about a public figure, says nothing, puts the flat of his hand on his own board and leaves it there while a barrow goes past, **and has not offered himself to anything since the hundred and fifty-seventh day of the Bare Month when he made it in a room and nobody in that room accepted it and nobody refused it.** In 0487 he withdraws that offer in his own mouth, in a market, for a reason nobody had thought of until he said it: **he cannot knock, and a man who sells off a board has a hand that has never been on a door, and if he went out onto those flats he would have come back with the same figure the first man has and had to say so in front of about nine people.** In 0489 he is not in the chapter at all and the chapter is about a fence and a handcart and a withy that has come loose. In 0490 his hand goes into that pocket and comes out with nothing in it, and it is flat on his coat over that pocket afterwards, and he does not take anything out of it and does not say one word. **He is not thanked in any of the five and he is not told he was right in any of the five, and the ninth line is not mentioned, moved, dated, read aloud, thanked for or resolved in any of them, and the two chalks are never in one hand in any of them.**

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-NINE WHO TRADES ON A BOARD — a board, nineteen rates on it, and a space where a name is not.** He is in three of the ten chapters. In 0485 he is right about a public figure in about the time it takes to say it, and nobody thanks him, nobody argues with him, nobody says he was right, and the chapter does not say he was right; **he took a name off that board in his own hand in about the ninth year of this flood and has never said so to anybody, the space is not filled, and about four people in that row looked at the face of that board that morning and not one of them looked at the space.** In the same chapter he is asked a question for the first time in as long as he has carried a board — whether a woman should say her prices out loud instead of chalking them — **and he says he does not know, and says that there is not one person in that market who does, and that he would rather say I do not know this morning than say something in the afternoon that turns out to be what he said this morning. That is what being right costs and it is paid in a morning.** In 0490 he comes down the row, reads the figure on the wall, says that a count of places read in a market reads as a count of the market and that is what a public figure does, and **nobody says he was right and nobody says he was wrong and he does not take his own board down and he goes on down the row and does not stop at the end of it the way he does.** In 0483 he is not in the chapter. **He asks the volume's new question on day 498 and does not answer it, and that day is not this batch's. He has not been defeated, is not converted, does not apologise, and the space on his board is still empty.**

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-EIGHT AT THE SALT WHARF — a tally-board, a knife, and a barrow.** He is in one chapter of the ten, 0488, and it is the only chapter of the ten in which a person works in weather with a stack higher than a man. He says one thing about a plank into a door frame and one thing about a man at the foot of the stack and neither is thanked, and **he has not taken that board down and put it up again and the tally-board still has about four hundred and forty on it. The other man of about thirty-eight is in none of these ten chapters and the two are still two people.**

**THE WOMAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-NINE WHO KEEPS A PUBLIC REGISTER — her second slate, a third column she leaves empty on purpose.** She is in four of the ten chapters and speaks in three of them and is thanked by nobody in any of them and no chapter says she is right. In 0482 she says that a room in this city is a thing a door makes and a date makes, and that about four people came up that stair who had not come up them before the water came, and that there is no instrument in four hundred miles for a room a person can walk into, and that she is not going to send them back down and is not going to write their names down either. In 0483 she says that the two figures on the two sides of that sheet are the same kind of thing, that the only person in four hundred miles who can say which of the two that office means is that office, that there is no day on either side of the page to say which went out first, and that she is not going to put either of them in a book and is not going to be the one who chooses. **In 0486 she asks the question that produces the name of this volume, once, in her own mouth, and gives her reason once, in her own mouth, and nobody improves on it and nobody asks her for it twice.** In 0490 she writes this city's own figure on the store's board in chalk out of the box on the step, a hand's width clear of the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day, and rubs nothing out. **The register is not opened on any of the ten days, the second column holds nothing on all of them, the third column is empty on purpose on all of them, and the third line is entered on day 493 and is not named in any of these ten. The second slate is named in one chapter of the ten and in that one it has a date on it and nothing under it, and no hand but hers goes near that shelf.** She is not Tamsin Quill and the two women of twenty-nine are never in one room.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE WHO CARRIES THINGS FOR A LIVING — a satchel with a flap.** He is in two of the ten chapters. In 0483 he turns the one-place sheet over and finds a second figure on the back of it in a second hand, and says once that a person can say the same about two numbers that are not equal without meaning anything false by it and that he has never in his life had to say the same about two numbers, **and that he has carried things up and down that road four times in a year and has not opened one of them and this was one of them and he did not open it.** He has still opened none of the seven things he has carried in three volumes. In 0487 he hears the man of about thirty-four withdraw his offer, says that is the end of it, that he is not going to say he was right or wrong, and that the two counts on that slate were made by two men and neither of them was him, and **says it with his back to the row and does not turn round while he says it.** Nobody thanked him in either chapter.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT SIXTY — a handcart, a hazel mallet whose head is bound in cord, and a coil of rope that has been used.** He is in one chapter of the ten, on the shut road out of this city, alone. He says out loud to a fence, with nobody on the road to hear it, **sixteen willow posts and eleven withies bound with the same cord, and that he put it up with a handcart and nobody sent him, and that the eleventh withy is loose at the top of the ninth post and he has looked at it four times and has not bound it up again and is not going to, and that there is nobody within four hundred miles of that lane who could come and tell him which two of those posts the argument is about.** He touches neither the mallet nor the rope. **He is not defeated, is not converted, is not a villain, is not happy, and whether the fence stands between the right two things is not settled in that chapter and is not asked about.**

**TAMSIIN QUILL — a folded sheet of road with about nine places on it, in the inside of her coat.** She is in one chapter of the ten, in a lane, and she does not take it out. She says once, in her own mouth, to a man's back and to a lane and to nobody else, **that a figure that says how many of something there are is the plainest instrument of that claim this flood has produced, and that it is plainer than a notice and plainer than a name, and that the figure is the one that counts and the name is the one that finds people.** Nobody improved on it, nobody asked her where she got it from, and the man she said it to did not answer her. **The consent fracture is not mended, no apology is made or refused or forgiven or asked to be forgiven in any of these ten, and the fifth condition is not given, and the register-keeper is not in that lane.**

**THE FIGURES WITH NO DESCRIPTOR, and no descriptor was attached to any of them, and this is the same convention the two batches behind this one used.** A woman at a stall two along from the store, who is in Chapters 0481, 0483, 0485 and 0490 and is given no number in any of them and **is the one who puts a pail down under her own board and has not carried water up that row since**. A woman at a stall two along, in 0487, who turns to say something to the man beside her and does not. A man at the end of the row, in 0481, 0485, 0487 and 0490. A man at the far end of that row, in 0485. A woman at the back of that room, in 0482, 0483 and 0486. A man at the back of that room, in 0486. A man at the bench, in 0483. A man at a door, in 0484. A man with a stick, in 0484. **NINE DESCRIBED FIGURES ACROSS THE TEN, none of them with a number, none of them thanked, and none of them in the plan's cast. Two of them are the same woman by description and are not stated to be the same woman and nothing in these ten chapters turns on whether they are.**

### 2. The people who are not in these ten days and were not expected to be

**THE WOMAN WHO WALKED A MARKET ON TWO FEET IS IN CHAPTER 0482 AND IS AT ZERO IN THE DESCRIPTOR MAP, with no name, no number, no descriptor and no age. She is asked nothing by anybody, she is not thanked, she is not guessed at, she is not improved on, and the woman who keeps the register did not write her down and did not ask her for it.** The man of about thirty-four had said he would go out and make the other figure himself, and she is in neither of the two chapters in which that offer is spoken about. **She asks the volume's new question on day 498 to one man and he does not answer it and the room does not hear it, and that day is the next batch's and not this one's.** The man of about fifty-seven is in NONE of these ten chapters, nobody asks him anything because he is not in them, and the length of new rope is not named in any of them. **The man of about thirty-nine's offer and his withdrawn offer are both about a walk and neither of them is her walk.** The man of about fifty-two who digs, the man of about fifty-nine who walks the four hundred miles, the man of about sixty-three, the man of about sixty-four who digs somebody else's ditch about nine hundred paces off, the woman of about fifty-two, the woman of about sixty-nine and the woman of about fifty-four are all in none of these ten chapters, and the woman of about sixty-nine does not ask a fourth question and no chapter of these ten remarks on her, and the woman of about fifty-four's unpaid attribution is still unpaid. The girl of seventeen, the keeper of the Redroot gate and the Regent are at ZERO on both readings, and *seventeen*, *Redroot* and *Regent* are at zero in all ten files.

### 3. The instrument rules that bind every name and every face above

**NO NAME AND NO PLACE ARRIVED BY NARRATION IN THESE TEN CHAPTERS. EVERY CARD NAMED THE DESCRIPTOR OF EVERY PERSON IN IT, NO CARD COULD NAME ONE FROM THE PLAN'S TABLES, AND NO CHAPTER INVENTED A PERSON. THE POOL IS EMPTY MEASURED ON ALL FOUR HUNDRED AND FIFTY FILES, AND THE NINE INCIDENTAL FIGURES WHO CARRY NO NUMBER ARE NAMED ABOVE AND ARE NOT IN THE CAST. THE DESCRIPTOR MAP ON THESE TEN FILES RETURNS TWENTY-THREE OCCURRENCES OF EIGHT DESCRIPTORS, AND EVERY ONE OF THE EIGHT IS A PERSON WHO ALREADY HAD ONE.**

**AND THE TWO THINGS THIS BATCH'S OWN INSTRUMENTS GOT WRONG, published because the same failure has happened in every batch of this volume: a duplication check at one threshold returned a clean batch with nine clones in it and was run at two thresholds; and the long-sentence instrument's first version implemented only reading A of the published definition and reported a batch mean of sixty-five words to a sentence in a batch whose real mean on that reading was thirty-three, and the difference is a closing quotation mark.** A marker that cannot see is an instrument and not a filter, the object inventory's third column is a plan and not a record, and every departure from it is published in the batch record and none was repaired.

**AND THE CHARACTER-PROPERTY RULE, WHICH THE REVIEW NAMED AND WHICH THIS BATCH OBSERVED RATHER THAN ASSUMED: every check in this volume tests a count, an object or a prohibition, and not one of them tests whether a record agrees with a page about a thing that belongs to a person.** The one property this batch held hardest is the second slate. **It is named in ONE chapter of the ten and in that one chapter it has a date on it and nothing under the date, and it is not picked up, not turned over and not written on, and no hand but the register-keeper's goes near that shelf on any of the ten days, and it is a different object from the register-keeper's own working slate, which lies face up on the bench in Chapter 0486.** The batch behind this one named the second slate in six of its nine and a review repair pass on that batch called six a verbal tic; this batch named it in one of its ten, and the difference is the two batches' distances from the room and not a rule anybody wrote.

**AND THE ONE THING NO FILE THIS BATCH WROTE MAY PRINT: the name of Volume 09 and the words the man of about thirty-four says in Chapter 0428; the volume's one rule, said on day 438; the answer spoken in Chapter 0357; the refusal spoken in Chapter 0393, whose six words also stand at `bible/power-system.md:1684` and at `outline/volume-08.md:208`; the day-154 sentence; and the words the man of about twenty-seven speaks in Chapter 0486. NAMED WHERE EACH OF THEM IS, PRINTED NONE OF THEM, NO ABSENCE CERTIFIED. THE HOLD IS ON THIS BATCH'S OWN FILES AND NOT ON WHERE A STRING STANDS: a plan is not a page, a permanent witness section is not a page, and a batch record is not a page either.**

## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0004 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS, and this block is at the foot of this file and it wins over every block above it

**A reviewer returned eight blocking findings on the ten pages and eleven on the batch record, and eight chapters changed by a sentence, a figure, an attribution or a closing paragraph. No chapter was restarted, no day moved, no card edited, nothing under `outline/` touched, no person created, no descriptor taken, and no eighth descriptor. Below is only the state of the people that the block above got wrong.**

### 1. What each person did, corrected

**ADRIAN VALE — Stage 2, in 0482, 0485 and 0488 and in no chapter in which he reads a page, unchanged by the repair.** His hands are on a door's edge, a bar and a piece of slate in 0482; on the frame of a barrow of stall boards and the flags in 0485; and on the end of a plank and the frame of a shed door in 0488. **THE THING HE CAUSES IN 0485 IS CORRECTED IN THE BLOCK ABOVE: a barrow goes out along a row and back and a man has to walk round the end of it, and a man of about thirty-four says one true thing about a board and is thanked by nobody, and a woman at a stall asks a question and is told nobody knows. It is not a standing offer spent; the offer is closed in 0487 and he is not in it.** He says nothing at all in all three and the batch contains no quoted word of his.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO KEEPS A STALL — a piece of chalk he does not use, in his own inside breast pocket, and a board on a string with a stone on the foot of it.** **IN 0485 HE NOW SAYS ONE THING, AND HE IS IN FIVE CHAPTERS AND THE FIFTH IS NOT A CHAPTER HE IS SILENT IN. He said it out loud, to the row and not to the man with the board under his arm, and it took him about as long as it takes to say it: that there is a gap in the writing on that man's own board and that it cannot be seen from where he stands. Nobody thanked him for it and nobody argued with him about it. The man of about thirty-nine turned the face of his own board towards himself, looked at the gap, put it back the way it was and went on down the row. THE CHAPTER DOES NOT SAY HE WAS RIGHT. HE ALSO HEARD THE WHOLE OF WHAT THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-NINE SAID AND SAID NOTHING BACK TO IT.** In 0487 he closes his own offer in his own mouth, in a market, for a reason nobody had thought of until he said it: **he cannot knock, and he has not knocked at a door since he was a boy in that market, and he has got a board of his own on a string, and if he went out onto those flats he would have stood in a gateway and counted and come back with the same figure the first man has and had to say so in front of about nine people. THE CLAUSE THE BLOCK ABOVE QUOTES — a man who sells off a board has a hand that has never been on a door — IS OFF THE PAGE, because it described a man who is not in that chapter.** In 0490 his hand goes into that pocket and comes out with nothing in it, and it is flat on his coat over that pocket afterwards, and he says no word. **He is not thanked in any of the five and he is not told he was right in any of the five, and the two chalks are never in one hand in any of them.**

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-NINE WHO TRADES ON A BOARD — a board, nineteen rates on it, and a space where a name is not.** **HIS OWN FOUR SECONDS ARE ON DAY 490 AND NOT ON DAY 485, AND THE BLOCK ABOVE IS CORRECTED. In 0485 he now says his piece about what a figure on a wall anybody can read does to the people counted by it, nobody answers him, nobody improves on it, and the timing that made it a beat in that chapter is off the page.** **What that chapter now does to him is put somebody else in front of the space on his board: the man of about thirty-four said out loud that there is a gap in the writing and that it cannot be seen from where he stands, and the man of about thirty-nine turned the board and looked at it and put it back and went on down the row. He still has not said one word about the space and nobody has asked him.** He took a name off that board in his own hand in about the ninth year of this flood and has never said so to anybody, the space is not filled, and about four people in that row looked at the face of that board that morning and not one of them looked at the space. In the same chapter he is asked a question for the first time in as long as he has carried a board — whether a woman should say her prices out loud instead of putting them on — and he says he does not know, and says that there is not one person in that market who does, and that he would rather say I do not know this morning than say something in the afternoon that turns out to be what he said this morning. In 0490 he comes down the row, reads the figure on the wall, says that a count of places read in a market reads as a count of the market, and **nobody says he was right and nobody says he was wrong and he does not take his own board down and he goes on down the row and does not stop at the end of it the way he does. THAT IS WHERE HIS FOUR SECONDS ARE AND IT IS WHERE `outline/volume-10.md` §10 PUTS THEM.** He asks the volume's new question on day 498 and does not answer it, and that day is not this batch's. He is not defeated, is not converted, does not apologise, and the space on his board is still empty.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT SIXTY — a handcart, a hazel mallet whose head is bound in cord, and a coil of rope.** **Chapter 0489 NOW SAYS OF THE OTHER MAN WHO HAS SAID THAT FENCE OUT LOUD THAT HE HAS SAID IT TWICE AND WAS ASKED FOR IT NEITHER TIME, which is what `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0462.md` and `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0478.md` show; the block above said the man had not said it again and that is withdrawn.** The two of them have still never seen one another, and neither has ever given those figures to anybody who was going to write them down.

**THE WOMAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-NINE WHO KEEPS A PUBLIC REGISTER — her second slate, a third column she leaves empty, and the store's own chalk out of a box on the step.** **HER SECOND SLATE IS NAMED IN TWO CHAPTERS OF THE TEN AND NOT ONE, in 0482 and in 0483, and in both it has a date on it and nothing under the date, and in neither is it picked up, turned over or written on, and in neither room did any hand but hers go near that shelf.** On day 490 about four people read the board and about five of the nine who were standing at that end of it did not, and every one of the four who read it worked out that the two sets of figures do not agree, and none of the nine can say which set is right.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-SEVEN WHO WASHES THE STORE'S BOARD — unchanged by the repair, and the repair did not touch the page that matters.** He spoke once on day 486 and refused the wall on day 490, four days apart, and no chapter of the ten connects the two, no chapter has him say he knows what he said, and no chapter makes the figures of the two hundred and twenty-fourth day and the figures of this volume the same figures. **The batch record's first edition described what he said on day 486 in three words and that description is withdrawn, because `outline/volume-10.md` §6.5 chose not to describe the answer at all and a record that describes it goes further than the plan does.**

### 2. The figures with no descriptor, corrected

**A woman at a stall two along FROM THE MAN WHO KEEPS A STALL, who is in Chapters 0481, 0485, 0487 and 0490 and is given no number in any of them and is the one who puts a pail down under her own board and has not carried water up that row since, and the man who keeps the bucket has carried it up again on every morning after and has not asked her to, and the trough has not been dry once in the whole of this flood.** A woman further along that row, in 0485, who has to lift the foot of her own board off the flags to get the wheel of a barrow past. A man at the end of the row, in 0481, 0487 and 0490. A man at the far end of that row, in 0485. A woman at the back of that room, in 0482 and 0483. A man at the back of that room, in 0486. A man at the bench, in 0482 and 0483. **A WOMAN at a door, in 0484 — the block above has a man there and the file has a woman.** A man with a stick, in 0484. **NINE DESCRIBED FIGURES ACROSS THE TEN, none of them with a number, none of them thanked, and none of them in the plan's cast. The block above placed the stall woman in Chapter 0483, which is a room over a market and has no stall in it, and that is corrected. Two of the nine are the same woman by description and are not stated to be the same woman and nothing in these ten chapters turns on whether they are.**

**AND THE GEOMETRY, WHICH NOBODY HAD PUBLISHED AND WHICH THE REPAIR HAD TO FIX: THE STORE'S BOARD IS ON THE WALL AT THE NEAR END OF THAT ROW, THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR IS TWO STALLS ALONG FROM IT, AND THE WOMAN IS TWO ALONG FROM HIM. Three things in three stalls in that order, and `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0471.md:11` is the page that places her two along from him, and five anchors across 0481, 0485 and 0487 that had her two along from the store are now anchored to him, because two along from the store was his stall.**

### 3. The instrument rules, and one figure the block above got wrong

**THE DESCRIPTOR MAP ON THESE TEN FILES RETURNS TWENTY-FOUR OCCURRENCES OF EIGHT DESCRIPTORS AND NOT TWENTY-THREE, and the extra one is the man of about thirty-nine in the sentence in Chapter 0485 that says who looked at the gap in his own board. Every one of the eight is a person who already had one and there is no ninth.** The nine incidental figures who carry no number are named above and are not in the cast. The pool is empty measured on all four hundred and fifty files, and this batch took no descriptor for anybody.

**AND THE CHARACTER-PROPERTY RULE, and one figure in the block above is corrected: THE SECOND SLATE IS NAMED IN TWO CHAPTERS OF THE TEN, IN 0482 AND IN 0483, and in both it has a date on it and nothing under the date, and in neither is it picked up, not turned over and not written on, and no hand but the register-keeper's went near that shelf on any of the ten days, and it is a different object from the register-keeper's own working slate, which lies face up on the bench in Chapter 0486.** The batch behind this one named the second slate in six of its nine; this batch named it in two of its ten, and the difference is the two batches' distances from the room and not a rule anybody wrote. **A CHARACTER-PROPERTY CHECK THAT COUNTS CHAPTERS AND NOT PAGES IS THE FAILURE THIS REPAIR FOUND IN THIS BLOCK, AND IT IS PUBLISHED BECAUSE A LATER PHASE WILL MAKE THE SAME INSTRUMENT.**

**AND THE TWO THINGS THIS BATCH'S OWN INSTRUMENTS GOT WRONG, with the correction: a duplication check at one threshold returned a clean batch with clones in it and was run at two thresholds — at forty words, zero within the ten and zero against all four hundred and seventy-nine pre-existing files; at sixteen, zero within the ten and three chapters carrying a residual run against TEN distinct prior texts, and all three are required declarations, and the repair introduced no run at either threshold; and the long-sentence instrument's first version implemented only reading A of the published definition and reported a mean far above the one reading A returns, and the difference is a closing quotation mark.** A marker that cannot see is an instrument and not a filter, the object inventory's third column is a plan and not a record, and every departure from it is published in the batch record and none was repaired.

## VOLUME 10, BATCH 0005 — Chapters 0491 to 0500, and this block is at the foot of this file and it wins over every block above it

**Twenty occurrences of five descriptors across ten chapters, and no sixth, and no person was created and no descriptor was taken for anybody. Below is the state of the people after these ten days, and the fifteen figures who carry no number are in §2 and the instrument figures are in §3.**

### 1. What each person did in these ten days

**ADRIAN VALE — Stage 2, in 0491, 0494 and 0496 and in no chapter in which he reads a page.** His hands are on a string, a stone, a board's lower rail and a length of split wood in 0491; on a tarpaulin, a batten and the top of a stack in 0494; and on a plank across a channel of wet silt in 0496. **IN 0491 A MAN'S BOARD HANGS A HAND'S WIDTH HIGHER THAN HE SET IT AND THAT MAN SPENDS THE DAY REACHING FOR THE FOOT OF HIS OWN BOARD WITH ONE HAND AND GETS A WET BAND ON HIS SLEEVE, and nobody at that end of that market knows whose hands did it. IN 0494 A STACK GOES OVER ONTO THE END BOARDS AND NOT ONTO THE RUN OF BOARDS A MAN HAS BEEN WALKING ROUND A BUILDING TO GET AT, and that man walks the cleared run with his hand on the top of each board and does not go round the shed once. IN 0496 A BARROW WITH A SLATE ON IT GOES OVER A CHANNEL A BARROW COULD NOT CROSS, and the count on the far side of eleven miles of flats is one slate's worth of doorways further on.** None of the three is a decision, a figure, a document, a notice or the panel. He says nothing at all in all three and the batch contains no quoted word of his. Nobody thanks him, nobody tells him he was right, nobody is waiting to see whether he is useful, and he is not useful to them. **0494 is one of the four chapters in the whole volume in which nobody asks him anything, and he works a thing out about the way a stack falls and the chapter does not say he was right.** He performs no working, no threshold is opened, the other world is not named once, nobody offers him a workway, he asks for none, and he is aged nowhere.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO KEEPS A STALL — a piece of chalk he does not use, in his own inside breast pocket, a board on a string with a stone on the foot of the string.** **HE IS IN THREE CHAPTERS AND HE IS SILENT IN NONE OF THEM. In 0491 his board hangs a hand's width higher than he set it because a stone at the foot of his string has been lifted out of the water and a length of split wood put under the near corner; he looks at it, pulls the rail, it does not come down, and he says he is not going to go down that row and find out who did and that he will reach for it the way he has reached for everything on that row for the whole of this flood. He does not say he is right and nobody says he is wrong. In 0495 HE TOOK THE BOARD DOWN OFF THE STRING AND DID NOT PUT IT UP AGAIN, the face of it went into the back of his own trestle, the string and the stone stayed on the post with nothing at the end of the string, and he said that a board on a string was the only thing at that end of that market a person could read from the far end of it and that he had taken it down because a figure went up on a wall and everything anybody walks past can read now, and that he had no answer for twelve people asking what a figure is. In 0500 his hand did not go into his coat at all that morning.** His piece of chalk did not come out in 0491, in 0495 or in 0500. Nobody thanked him in any of the three and nobody argued with him.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-NINE WHO TRADES ON A BOARD — a board, nineteen rates on it, and a space where a name is not.** **HIS OWN FOUR SECONDS ARE ON DAY 490 AND NOT ON ANY DAY IN THIS BATCH, and this batch gave him no four-second beat at all. In 0498 HE IS ASKED ONE QUESTION BY ONE WOMAN AND HE DOES NOT ANSWER IT, he stands with his hand along the top rail of his own board and does not look at her and does not look at the room, and after about as long as it takes a man to decide whether a person is going to say anything else he puts his thumb on the space on that board where a name is not and takes his hand off it again. Nobody thanked her for asking and nobody told her it was a good question. In 0500 he comes down that row, reads the third figure on the wall, and goes on the length of the row and does not stop at the end of it the way he does.** He is not thanked, is not argued with, is not defeated, does not apologise, and the space on his board is still empty and still unasked. The woman who put a face-down sheet of paper with a copy of that figure on it in the room over a market on day 499 is not named and is not this man.

**THE WOMAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-NINE WHO KEEPS A PUBLIC REGISTER — her second slate, her third column empty on purpose, her own working slate with two figures on one face of it, and the store's own chalk out of a box on the step.** **IN 0493 SHE OPENED THE REGISTER, WROTE THE THIRD LINE, SAID WHAT IT WAS OUT LOUD BEFORE THE PEN WENT NEAR THE PAGE, AND WAS THE PERSON WHO TOOK THE BOOK OFF THE SHELF AND THE PERSON WHO PUT IT BACK, and no hand but hers went near that shelf on any of the ten days. In 0500 SHE CHALKED A THIRD SET OF FIGURES ON THAT BOARD, said what it was before anybody said anything, and was thanked by nobody and told by nobody that she was right. HER SECOND SLATE IS NAMED IN FOUR OF THE TEN CHAPTERS — 0492, 0493, 0498 and 0499 — IN FOUR DIFFERENT SETS OF WORDS, AND IN EVERY ONE OF THE FOUR IT HAS A DATE ON IT AND NOTHING UNDER IT AND IS NOT PICKED UP AND NOT TURNED OVER AND NOT WRITTEN ON.** In 0497 and 0496 her own working slate is the object on a barrow across eleven miles of flats, and neither figure on it is printed in either chapter.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-EIGHT AT THE SALT WHARF — a tally-board, a knife with a blunt edge, a bar, and a shed door that will not shut.** He is in Chapter 0494 and in no other chapter of these ten. He walked to the head of a fallen stack and back, said that it had gone the way a stack goes, along what it is made of, and that he was not going to thank a man for a batten and was not going to say a word about it to the man who put it there, and that he had watched about nine of them come down on that wharf and had not watched one of them decide anything about where it was going. He said it is on the end boards and it has not moved a foot and neither have I. He did not go round the shed once while it was cleared. Nobody thanked him and nobody argued with him. The figure on his tally-board is printed nowhere in these ten.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE WHO CARRIES THINGS FOR A LIVING — a satchel with a flap, a barrow, and the register-keeper's own working slate face up under a fold of saking.** He carried four things up that road in a year and did not open one of them, and this was the fifth and it is not in a satchel, and he said out loud that he was not going to look at the slate on the way and that it was not his to be the first man to say. He kept his hand over the face of the slate while he counted and moved it for each one. He said that the difference between the two figures was a place about two thirds of the way along the line, and that he was not going to be the man who went back and looked for it again. Nobody thanked him and nobody argued with him.

**THE WOMAN WHO WALKED A MARKET ON TWO FEET, WHO HAS STOPPED, WHO HAS NO AGE AND NO NUMBER AND NO DESCRIPTOR ON ANY PAGE.** **SHE IS IN ONE CHAPTER OF THESE TEN AND IN NO OTHER — Chapter 0498 — and she comes up that stair with a slate of her own under her arm and no tin in her hands and stands at the back with her back to the wall, and nobody in that room asks her one thing about anything at all. SHE ASKS ONE QUESTION OF ONE MAN AND NOT OF THE ROOM, and nobody thanks her for it and nobody tells her it was a good question or a bad one, and about four of the nine people in that room had a thing they could have said to a woman at the back of it and said nothing. She gets nothing back. She is given no name, no number, no descriptor and no age in that chapter or in any of the ten.** THREAD, OPEN.

### 2. The fifteen figures who carry no number, and the instrument

**A woman at a stall two along from the man of about thirty-four, in 0491 and 0495, who has a pail under her own board and who says that somebody has put a piece of wood under his board and that she would rather have a hand in the air than the other way round, and who is anchored to him in both chapters and not to the store. A man at the end of that row, in 0491 and 0500. A woman at a stall, in 0500, who says the thing that is said to the flags. A man at the far end of that row, in 0495, who comes down the row, looks at a post and a bare back of a board, and goes on without stopping. A man at the bench, in 0492, 0493 and 0499. A man further down that bench, in 0493. A woman at the back, in 0492 and 0499. A woman at a door, in 0497. A man with a spade, in 0496, digging somebody else's ditch about nine hundred paces off, not paid, not asked. A man in a wet coat going back along the near bank, in 0496. A man who sat down on a door-sill, in 0497. A man on the far end of a line, in 0497. A man at a door, in 0492. A man at the far end of a bench, in 0499. FOURTEEN ACROSS THE TEN, none of them with a number, none of them thanked, and none of them in the plan's cast, and a man at the bench and a man further down that bench are not stated to be the same man.**

**THE DESCRIPTOR MAP ON THESE TEN FILES RETURNS TWENTY OCCURRENCES OF FIVE DESCRIPTORS — man of about thirty-four at five, woman of about twenty-nine at four, man of about thirty-eight at four, man of about thirty-one at four, man of about thirty-nine at three — and no sixth. Every one of the five is a person who already had one. The pool is empty measured on all four hundred and fifty files and this batch took no descriptor for anybody. The man of about fifty-seven, the man of about sixty, the man of about sixty-three, the man of about sixty-four, the man of about fifty-nine, the man of about fifty-two who digs, the man of about forty-one, the woman of about fifty-two, the woman of about sixty-nine, the woman of about fifty-four, the girl of seventeen, the keeper of the Redroot gate, the Regent, and Tamsin Quill are all at ZERO in these ten chapters.**

### 3. The character-property rule, and the two figures that came back non-zero

**THE SECOND SLATE IS NAMED IN FOUR CHAPTERS OF THE TEN AND IN FOUR DIFFERENT SETS OF WORDS, and in every one of the four it has a date on it and nothing under it, and in none is it picked up, not turned over and not written on, and no hand but the register-keeper's went near that shelf on any of the ten days. IT IS A DIFFERENT OBJECT FROM THE REGISTER-KEEPER'S OWN WORKING SLATE, and in Chapter 0493 both of them are on the page in the same chapter and are named as two, and the working slate is on a bench in that chapter and on a barrow across eleven miles of flats in Chapters 0496 and 0497.** THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR'S OWN PIECE OF CHALK AND THE STORE'S OWN CHALK ARE NEVER IN ONE HAND, and a third piece of chalk appears in an unnamed man's hand at a wall in Chapter 0499 copying a figure onto a sheet of paper, and no chapter of the ten puts two of the three in one hand.

**AND THE TWO FIGURES THAT CAME BACK NON-ZERO, PUBLISHED RATHER THAN PASSED: *level* is at ONE, in Chapter 0491, in the sentence that says water finds its own level along the flags, and the volume's own rule makes the exception for an ordinary English word about water and asks for the figure. *rooms* is at ONE, in Chapter 0493, in a general reference to two different rooms and not a count of rooms for anybody. *notice* and *notices* are at ZERO in all ten and the card at the head of the batch record said one of them would stand and the departure is published there. A marker that cannot see is an instrument and not a filter: the *board* marker returns 57 occurrences across the ten files case-sensitive and 81 case-insensitive, and the store's board is a minority of them, and the case flag alone moved that row by twenty-four instances.**
